#97. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#96. How sporting events are being affected by Middle East conflict

The Qatar Grand Prix is scheduled to be held at the Lusail International Circuit between 27and 29 November.

The 2027 Asian Cup is just the latest event to be postponed in the Middle East amid concerns about missile attacks by Yemen's Houthi movement.

The 24-team tournament - Asia's version of the Euros - was scheduled to take place in Saudi Arabia from 7 January to 5 February next year.

The Asian Cup has now been delayed by almost a year, and will be held between 23 December, 2027 and 21 January, 2028.

Sport in the region had already been heavily affected by the US-Iran conflict, which led to the cancellation of the Bahrain and Saudi Arabian Formula 1 Grand Prix weekends in April this year.

Now the races in Qatar and Abu Dhabi at the end of the year are also in doubt due to that ongoing situation and the current conflict involving the Houthis. If they are cancelled, F1 would have lost four of its 24 events this year.

It is not just football and F1 that have been forced to cancel, reschedule or move high-profile events, and many more remain under threat.

BBC Sport takes a look at the situation across the sports.

The Saudi Pro League has, for the most part, managed to carry on as usual.

Several âdomestic â league games had to be postponed, including three fixtures last month as the situation escalated.

The games were moved in the immediate aftermath of the attack on a major airport in Riyadh - though the SPL said they were switched "as a result of mandatory minimum rest periods between matches and to accommodate logistical requirements".

Following the postponement of the Asian Cup, this weekend's full round of Saudi Pro League has been called off.

The league once again has not attributed this to conflict in the region, instead saying the calendar would be recalibrated after the cancellation of the Asian Cup to achieve "a more balanced distribution of fixtures".

The AFC Champions League has been affected as well.

Cristiano Ronaldo and Kingsley Coman were among Al-Nassr's star players who did not to fly to Uzbekistan this week "given the pressure of matches and the amount of travel involved".

Al-Wasl of the United Arab Emirates also opted not to fly to Saudi Arabia to play Al-Nassr, and the match will be rearranged.

Meanwhile, the wife of Ollie Watkins, who plays for Riyadh-based Al-Hilal, posted on TikTok that she would be staying in the UK with their two children "until it calms down".

"We've had a bit of a plot twist," said Ellie Watkins. "I have come back to the UK for a little bit.

"There's just a little bit of conflict and things going on over in Saudi and we basically decided just to stay here.

"I felt like I needed to do that for the kids so there is some form of normality until we can get back out there and all be together."

Other well-known stars playing in the country include Gabriel Martinelli, Tijjani Reijnders, Darwin Nunez, Ivan Toney, Alexandre Lacazette, Ruben Neves, Sadio Mane and Joao Felix.

The United Arab Emirates is due to hold an Formula One race next month

Formula 1 has a major dilemma about racing in the Middle East both at the end of this season and the beginning of next.

Most pressingly, the 2026 season is due to end with races in Qatar on 27-29 November and Abu Dhabi on 4-6 December.

As things stand, the sport is pressing ahead with plans to go to those races after being given security reassurances from various governmental sources that it should be safe.

There are, though, many people within the sport with misgivings about going.

Should the situation in the Middle East deteriorate in the next week or so, they could be called off and replaced with a single event in Imola in Italy on the Abu Dhabi weekend.

The 2027 season is scheduled to start in Bahrain on 12-14 March, with Saudi Arabia hosting on the following weekend.

But as things stand between Saudi Arabia and the Yemeni Houthi separatists, that race could not go ahead, and is likely to be cancelled if nothing changes by December.

Bahrain and Saudi Arabia are linked by a causeway, at the Saudi end of which is a facility belonging to state oil company Aramco, which has been targeted by the Houthis, while it joins Bahrain close to the F1 track.

One option being discussed is to hold that race in Malaysia, as was done in October this year.

The Dakar Rally was due to take place in Saudi Arabia in January but has been moved "following recent developments in the region".

Now the 2027 edition will begin in Loule-Faro in Portugal and end in Marrakech in Morocco.

Saudi Arabia had hosted the previous seven editions of Dakar, and the intention is to return in 2028.

Rally Saudi Arabia, part of the 2026 FIA World Rally Championship, which was scheduled for 12â15 November, has been cancelled.

But MotoGP will still take place this weekend at the Lusail International Circuit in Doha, Qatar, with three races left in the season.

It will go ahead without the Yamaha team, which has been advised against travelling from Japan.

Novak Djokovic is due to take part in the Six Kings Slam

The Six Kings Slam, which should have begun on Saturday in Riyadh, Saudi Arabia, has been postponed by two months.

It is now scheduled to run from 27 December to 30 December at the ANB Arena, located about 31km away from the King Khalid International Airport, which was the target of an attack on 10 October that killed 13 people and injured 308 others.

Novak Djokovic, Alexander Zverev, Carlos Alcaraz, Alex de Minaur, Taylor Fritz and Learner Tien are due to compete.

But the 2026 WTA Finals, this weekend's season-ending championship of the women's tour, will not take place in Saudi Arabia.

A mutual agreement was reached to terminate a hosting agreement early, and it will instead take place at Indian Wells in the United States.

Saudi Arabia regularly hosts title fights and high-profile bouts.

Only last week, Mexican legend Canelo Alvarez reclaimed the WBC super middleweight championship against Christian Mbilli.

But the whole of the card did not survive as Lindolfo Delgado withdrew from his IBF lightweight title fight against Arthur Biyarslanov over safety concerns.

Next on the schedule is the Zuffa Boxing event, which will feature Carlos Adames against Welshman Gerome Warburton in Riyadh Season 13, on 14 November.

The WWE Crown Jewel 2026 event will still take place this weekend, but at a new venue.

It was originally scheduled for the Kingdom Arena, before being switched to the ANB arena.

The reigning men's and women's world champions of WWE's Raw and SmackDown face each other.

Roman Reigns, Sami Zayn, Liv Morgan, Chelsea Green, Bron Breakker, Austin Theory, Penta, Je'Von Evans, Cody Rhodes and Randy Orton are on the bill.

The FIFAe Finals, an esports tournament series organised by Fifa, should have taken place in Jeddah last month but was cancelled.

This week's UCI Urban Cycling World Championships, for the world's leading riders in trials and BMX Freestyle, in Riyadh was called off too, with a decision due "on the sporting consequences of this announcement".

The PIF Saudi International event, the season finale of the Asian Tour, will not be held between 18-21 November at Riyadh Golf Club.

After "an assessment of the organisational and logistical challenges associated with hosting the tournament" the decision was taken to call it off.

And on Tuesday, the Professional Triathletes Organisation said it was moving the Saudi Arabia T100 and World Triathlon Cup, in Jeddah, from the end of this month to 16-17 April.

#95. French blood banks hacked in latest cyber-attack on Europe

Temperature and defrosting thresholds raised to ‘very high levels’ at three cold stores stocking vital blood products

France’s national blood service has said hackers accessed control systems and boosted temperatures at three cold stores stocking vital blood products, in an apparent escalation of recent cyber-attacks on fridges and freezers across Europe.

The French blood service (EFS) said temperature and defrosting thresholds had been raised to “very high levels” in the attack, adding that its alarm systems had allowed technicians to intervene before the products were compromised.

The EFS is France’s public blood transfusion operator, collecting, testing, preparing and distributing products such as blood, plasma and platelets to more than 1,900 hospitals around the country. It supports more than a million patients a year.

The attack, which EFS said had occurred overnight on Monday, came as the temperatures of refrigerators and freezers in supermarkets in almost a dozen European countries soared as high as 50C (122F), causing millions of euros of food damage.

Retailers in Italy, Germany, Romania and the UK were targeted on Tuesday, after Belgium, Estonia, Ireland, Finland, Luxembourg, the Netherlands and Sweden reported problems with their refrigeration systems the previous day.

In a wave of attacks in France last month, hackers seized control of cooling units in as many as 700 supermarkets. A regional branch of France’s Crédit Mutuel bank said on Wednesday it had been the victim of a cyber-attack.

Something similar may have happened in the US in early August, when a series of mysterious fridge malfunctions hit commissaries in at least six military bases. The Pentagon is investigating a suspected hack.

After Italy’s government held emergency talks with intelligence officials on Tuesday, Thomas Regnier, an EU spokesperson, said the bloc’s agencies were holding urgent discussions with national authorities to formulate a collective response.

“These are not isolated incidents and of course we have to be collectively resilient and prepared for such cyber incidents,” he said, declining to name any potential culprits. No motive for the attacks is known and no one has claimed responsibility.

A growing range of devices, including commercial refrigeration systems, can be monitored and controlled remotely. Without patching to repair emerging vulnerabilities, internet-connected software can leave code open to hackers.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

Some authorities and experts fear government-sponsored hybrid attacks ​in the aftermath of Russia’s 2022 invasion of Ukraine. Western intelligence officials say Moscow is waging a campaign of sabotage in Europe that is taking many forms.

Rafe Pilling, the senior director for threat intelligence at the cybersecurity company Sophos, told the Guardian this week the most plausible attribution scenarios were a “state-based signalling and demonstration of capability” or a lone-wolf hacker.

EFS said it was not directly targeted, with the attacks hitting cold stores run by its contractors. Products stored in the affected rooms, which included plasma bags for transfusions and medicine manufacturing, had been moved to other sites.

It added that France’s national cybersecurity agency (ANSSI) and the health ministry had been alerted, and monitoring of all its equipment had been reinforced.

#94. EU to lead talks on support for Ukraine with US, Nato, partners – Europe live

School in Komyshuvakha in the Zaporizhzhia region was struck on Monday

With most eyes still on the US this morning, Brussels is quietly getting on with the task of leading on discussions about the next round of support for Ukraine.

The European Commission will hold talks with the US, France, Germany, Britain, Nato, and the World Bank, looking what can be done to help Kyiv throughout the winter and how to support the US-led peace process with the upcoming negotiations in Abu Dhabi.

Trump’s special envoy Steve Witkoff said US negotiators would meet with their European partners in Brussels.

Their talks come after another night of heavy attacks on Ukraine, hitting power stations in Kyiv and Zhytomyr and a cargo vessel at sea near Odesa.

I will also bring you any European reactions to the midterms in the US, although many politicians (while quietly amused or relieved) may choose to stay silent to not risk Donald Trump’s wrath.

It’s Wednesday, 4 November 2026, it’s Jakub Krupa here, and this is Europe Live.

Meanwhile, Ukraine’s president Volodymyr Zelenskyy – fresh back from his meetings in Germany yesterday and today – has offered an update on the search and rescue operations after the Russian strike on a school in Komyshuvakha in the Zaporizhzhia region on Monday.

40 people have been confirmed dead, with fears that more than 30 may still be under the rubble, Zelenskyy said.

“Unfortunately, there has been very little reaction from the world to this insane attack. But it, like thousands upon thousands of other war crimes Russia has committed against Ukraine and Ukrainians, demands a strong response. Evil always goes further if it is left unanswered.”

German foreign minister Johann Wadephul has said the results of the US midterm elections will mean some “continuity” in Germany’s relations with the Trump administration, but also a “change” in ties with the US Democrats as he hoped to see some breakthrough on Ukraine.

Berlin will “continue to seek common ground” with the Trump administration, “striving for compromise” on key issues in the face of global challenges.

But these elections “also signal change” as the Democratic Party’s success – which he noted was “of a magnitude rare in American history” – will “likely seek to shift the political discourse in the US.”

He said he did not “any complications” arising from this result, and noted that the political engagement will now need to be “broader” and “perhaps even more interesting in certain aspects.”

He said many foreign policy issues featured prominently in the US elections, including tariffs and the war in Iran.

Wadephul added that he hoped for “more dialogue in Congress, across party lines, about what can be done collectively to support Ukraine.

“I think they’ve been holding back on that issue for quite some time ‌now during the election campaign,” he said.

“I also know that in both chambers – and within both parties – there are staunch supporters of Ukraine who clearly recognise that this Russian war of aggression threatens not only security and freedom in Europe but also in the United States.”

#93. Why Kim Kardashian came back to 'the worst TV drama ever'

'I'm not a quitter': Kim Kardashian on retaking the bar exam

It was meant to be Kim Kardashian's triumphant debut as a leading actor - but last year's legal drama All's Fair was so universally panned by critics that it actually broke streaming records as audiences clambered to see what all the fuss was about.

Kardashian, who is best known as a media personality and businesswoman, tells the BBC she had no choice but to "lean into" the discourse and come back for a second series.

"We thought we were this law show, dealing with some really serious issues," she jokes.

But viewers mostly enjoyed its comedy value - with outlandish outfits and zingy one-liners from co-stars Sarah Paulson and Glenn Close sending the show straight into the pantheon of 'camp classics'.

Kim Kardashian has returned to her role as Allura Grant in Ryan Murphy's legal drama All's Fair

"We're a full comedy now," the 46-year-old admits, adding: "The beauty in producing a show with writers that are really tuned in to pop culture is they can see what the response is and write accordingly to that."

All's Fair, which was created by American Horror Story and Glee showrunner Ryan Murphy, became the Hulu network's most-watched scripted series debut of the last three years and was immediately renewed.

Following a group of divorce lawyers - played by Kardashian, Close, Paulson, Naomi Watts and Niecy Nash-Betts - the show was panned for bad acting, with Kardashian bearing the brunt of criticism.

The Hollywood Reporter called her "wooden", and the Times declared that the show "may be the worst TV drama ever".

But the hallmark of Kardashian's career has been her ability to back herself - whether that's going from years of being snubbed by Met Gala organisers to becoming its most anticipated guest, or overcoming backlash to the launch of her shapewear company to turn it into a $5bn (Â£3.7bn) brand.

Kardashian says: "What is the alternative, right? What is the other choice that you have when you're getting criticised?

"Do you just fall in and feel that, or keep your head down and try better, try harder and try a different path?"

Having been in the spotlight for more than 20 years, she's "always had a really good sense of my mental health", and says that comes from being able to "brush off" those who seek to bring her down.

"I always do things that are tough because I genuinely want to learn," Kardashian says.

Kardashian's co-stars Sarah Paulson and Naomi Watts have also returned for season two

While she was promoting the legal drama last year, Kardashian was waiting to find out if she had passed exams to become a lawyer in real life, after six years of studying for an apprenticeship that negated the need for a university degree.

Shortly after the first series aired, she found out she had failed the exam - but says that won't be the end of her pursuit of the qualification.

"If I don't pass something, if I don't do the best at something, then I'll get there," she says. "I don't worry. I just have to keep going."

But after her admission that she had used AI for legal advice, external, she does say she will change how public she is about her studies.

"I'm going to be a little more quiet about my journey until I get there," says Kardashian, who has four children with rapper Kanye West and is now in a relationship with British Formula 1 driver Lewis Hamilton.

"I know there was a lot of talk out there like, 'she's given up', and that's funny to me.

"I like people to think what they want to think and I'll take it again and let you guys know once I've passed. I'll be very vocal about that," she says.

All's Fair was criticised in its first series for bad acting, but Kardashian's co-stars say she has stepped up in the next iteration of the show

Audiences have only been given a glimpse of what to expect from the second series of All's Fair so far.

Co-star Watts, who also serves as a producer on the show alongside Kardashian, says they have "doubled down" on "the high fashion, high drama and heightened reality" that audiences enjoyed the first time around.

As for Kardashian's acting, Watts says she's on a "trajectory" that sees her "stepping up in the second season".

"She was improvising, riffing, doing all kind of things that I thought were deeply impressive," Watts adds, to which Kardashian jokes: "I think I'm just naturally so funny."

One of the positives that critics did cite was the show's desire to spotlight the duality of professional women who were both feminine and successful, which Kardashian says she "loves" because it is "a really realistic depiction of a lot of women I know".

"It doesn't mean that just because you're super smart that you can't be fashionable and into glamorous things as well," she adds.

#92. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#91. Tears, celebrations and selfies: US midterms in pictures

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Hugs, cheers and smiles broke out as the Democrats picked up a string of victories in Republican strongholds, delivering them the Senate and the House.

The win gives them control of Congress for the rest of Donald Trump's presidency.

Meanwhile, it was a challenging night for Republican supporters, after a succession of losses in reliably Republican states.

Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms defeated Republican Rick Jackson, a billionaire businessman endorsed by President Trump.

Bottoms becomes the first black woman elected to be governor of Georgia.

The state has not had a Democratic governor in more than 20 years.

Republican Senator Jon Husted shed a tear whilst surrounded by his family after losing his Ohio seat to Democrat Sherrod Brown.

Another Democrat victory was seen in Georgia where Senator Jon Ossoff was re-elected, giving him a second term.

Speculation surrounds Ossoff, considered a potential leading contender to run for the Democrats in the next presidential election in 2028.

James Talarico, the new US senator in Texas, and Gina Hinojosa, the state's new governor, toasted their successes.

In Portland, Maine, supporters reacted to the news that Democratic Senate candidate Troy Jackson won his seat. He beat Republican veteran Susan Collins who has been in the Senate for nearly 30 years.

Susan Collins was pictured looking at election results coming in on a laptop.

The mood was different for Republican supporters at a midterm election night watch party in Des Moines, Iowa. Republican Ashley Hinson lost her Senate seat to Democrat Josh Turek.

Democrat Chris Pappas made history as the first openly gay man elected to the Senate, beating his rival Republican Senator John Sununu.

Pappas celebrated his New Hampshire win on stage with his husband.

Attendees paused for thought after a watch party for Maine Republican Senate candidate Susan Collins, following her defeat by Democrat Troy Jackson.

In North Carolina, the Democrats secured the night's first flip of a Senate seat.

The state's former Democratic governor, Roy Cooper, won against Republican candidate Michael Whatley.

A supporter raised a jubilant fist to celebrate the North Carolina win.

In Michigan, Democrat Abdul El-Sayed will become the first Muslim US senator. He told voters: "You took a chance on the unlikeliest of candidates."

The mood was subdued amongst Republican supporters, after a string of defeats in reliably Republican strongholds.

#88. Anthony Zurcher: Democrats push deep into Trump heartland on historic night

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Watch: The key moments from the US midterm elections

Democrats have torn up Donald Trump's political map on a night that will send shockwaves through American politics for years to come.

With a string of major Senate victories in states that have been reliably Republican for decades - and in Kansas's case, nearly a century - a blue tidal wave crashed across the nation, sweeping Republicans out of power.

Halfway through President Donald Trump's second term, the Democrats now have decisive control of the House of Representatives and have wrested a majority back in the Senate.

They will be able to frustrate Trump's agenda, and launch investigations into his administration. A historic third impeachment attempt could also be on the cards.

Few imagined such an outcome was possible in the months after Trump returned to the White House with unified Republican control of Congress in January 2025.

As the public mood turned against him and his party, Democrats had hopes of retaking the House.

But they contemplated a Senate map that appeared unfriendly, populated by states Trump had won comfortably just two years ago.

Instead, Democrats rewrote the political equations with victories in Texas, Maine, Kansas, Ohio, Iowa and North and South Carolina.

In comments to the BBC's US partner CBS, Trump pledged to strike deals with congressional Democrats, adding that he didn't want to have to spend his final two years in office rejecting their legislation.

He somewhat undermined that sentiment later, posting a picture to Truth Social of his trademark red baseball hat with the word "veto" emblazoned on it.

The president went on to note that his name wasn't on the ballot this year, but if it had been Republicans would have won a decisive victory.

Trump had helped elevate many of those Republican candidates, however, and had urged voters to pretend he was up for re-election.

REACTION: Trump accepts Democrats did a 'good job' in election

THE BIG MOMENTS: Ecstatic scenes that greeted key results

In the Senate, Democrats flipped at least six seats, comfortably securing a majority.

Adam Hamilton won in Kansas - a state that last elected a Democrat to the Senate almost 100 years ago during the Great Depression.

James Talarico beat Republican Ken Paxton in Texas' Senate race, ending a 38-year Democratic losing streak in statewide elections there.

In South Carolina, Annie Andrews beat Darline Graham, who had been appointed to the seat after the death of her brother, Senator Lindsey Graham. Few analysts had that seat in play even a few months ago.

Five-term Republican Susan Collins conceded Maine's Senate election to Democrat Troy Jackson.

Paralympian Josh Turek flipped the Iowa Senate seat held by retiring Republican Senator Joni Ernst. Democrats also wrested from Republicans a Senate seat in Ohio, with Sherrod Brown's comeback victory over Jon Husted.

Meanwhile, Democrat Abdul El-Sayed has beaten Republican Mike Rogers in Michigan's bitterly fought Senate race.

Nor was that the end of the electoral carnage for Republicans.

Ruby-red Texas witnessed another seismic result in the governor's race.

Democratic challenger Gina Hinojosa ousted the incumbent Republican Greg Abbott, who was earlier this year seen as the prohibitive favourite.

Democrats also took governors' races in Iowa, Ohio and Georgia, which made history by electing the first black woman to such a post.

Meanwhile, the party has won a House majority for the first time since 2022.

They are projected to win 245 House seats and the Republicans 166 seats.

Democrats celebrate in Maine where the party won a crucial Senate race

A look at the key races offers plenty of reasons why.

Democrats flipped House seats in Texas, North Carolina, Pennsylvania, Iowa, Kentucky and Colorado, among others. And they were poised to make gains in Virginia and Indiana.

Black Democrats in the southern states turned out in record number, helping to carry their party to victory. And Hispanic voters, whose support had helped secure Trump's presidential victory in 2024, swung decisively back to the Democrats.

Democrats won all three House races in the overwhelmingly Hispanic congressional districts along the Texas-Mexico border.

And Hispanic voters helped Democrats hold House seats in Florida that the state's Republicans had sought to flip by redrawing the congressional district lines.

In fact, the congressional redistricting battles that had dominated headlines for months last year ended up being a bust for the Republicans.

In Texas, they had hoped their line-drawing would net five new seats for their party. But on Tuesday they may end up losing one seat.

If there is a silver lining for conservatives, Florida as a whole still appears to tilt to the right.

Republican Senator Ashley Moody narrowly won re-election, as did congressman Byron Donalds, who was elected the Sunshine State's first black governor.

And embattled Republicans were able to hold governorships in Vermont and New Hampshire. But the bright spots were few and far between.

Just two years ago, Trump won the presidency with 48% of the vote. He claimed an electoral mandate.

He then went about governing like it, pushing sometimes unpopular policies on trade and immigration, and launching an Iran war that is opposed by most of the American public.

On Tuesday, voters gave him the kind of stinging rebuke that will not be easily forgotten.

BBC asks voters across US what brought them to the polls today

#86. Kate McClymont questioned over ‘nail in the coffin’ and poo emoji texts at Alan Jones trial

Award-winning investigative journalist says she ‘probably’ texted an alleged victim’s mother saying ‘I hope the shit does hit the fan for him’, court hears

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Investigative journalist Kate McClymont has told a Sydney court that an audio recording in which she said new information would be “an absolute nail in the coffin” did not refer to “killing” Alan Jones.

In a phone call with the mother of the youngest alleged victim of Jones, which was played in court, the Sydney Morning Herald’s chief investigative reporter expressed excitement when the woman said her son had spoken to police in the same year as the alleged assault.

“This would be an absolute nail in the coffin that police were involved,” McClymont said in the call. “That’s just brilliant. Oh, I’m breathless.”

Asked by defence barrister Gabrielle Bashir SC if she meant it was a nail in the coffin of her client, McClymont said no.

“You meant the nail in the coffin of Alan Jones,” Bashir suggested.

“It doesn’t mean killing Mr Jones,” she said. “It is the nail in the coffin for the story.”

The court heard last month that Jones allegedly put his tongue inside the mouth of the schoolboy, known as complainant A, and touched him on the bottom when he stayed overnight at the former 2GB broadcaster’s Fitzroy Falls farm.

“I felt violated and I felt gross,” the witness, who began corresponding with Jones as a 13-year-old, said in the witness box.

A is one of five men who allege Jones indecently assaulted or sexually touched them between 2003 and 2020.

Jones has pleaded not guilty to 21 counts of sexual touching and indecent assault.

During her cross-examination, McClymont said “nail in the coffin” referred to new information gleaned from complainant A’s mother.

She said after she published her investigation into Jones in December 2023, police had told her “we have received no complaints about Alan Jones and we have not looked at him”.

But when she spoke to the mother she discovered that her son did speak to police and it was a “significant event” for the story.

The court heard McClymont was put in contact with the detective who had spoken to the-then teenager in the principal’s office at his school.

Bashir asked the journalist if she told the mother that both the NSW police minister’s office and the police sex crimes unit said that, should the case be re-opened, her son would not be identified because he was under 18.

The award-winning journalist agreed that she had sent A’s mother texts including: “All I wanted to say is Alan Jones is the subject of a police investigation!!!!” and “I hope the shit does hit the fan for him”, with two poo emojis.

The reporter agreed that she texted a contact from the police media team “Can hardly wait!” in reference to the impending arrest of Jones.

On the day of Jones’s arrest at his harbourside apartment in Circular Quay, McClymont texted her contact to clarify details, saying “beauty” when the arrest was confirmed.

The journalist agreed she provided a statement to police about Jones in June 2024 and continued to report because she had been given “no indication” that she would be a prosecution witness. She told the court she stopped reporting on the case when she was told she would be a witness at the trial.

McClymont will return to court on Thursday for a third day.

The trial before judge Glenn Walsh in the Downing Centre local court continues.

#83. Everything you need to know from the US midterm elections

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Watch: Key moments from the US midterm election results

Democrats have won both the US Senate and House of Representatives, taking control of Congress for the rest of Donald Trump's presidency.

Projected results have provided the party with a number of things to celebrate - and rival Republicans some reasons to commiserate.

Here is everything you need to know about some of the big Democratic wins and some historic firsts.

Democrats have tallied up victories in states that were comfortably won by Trump two years ago in the presidential election.

With James Talarico winning a seat in the US Senate seat and Gina Hinojosa being elected state governor, Democrats scored two statewide victories in Texas - ending a string of defeats they have endured for almost two generations.

James Talarico, the new US senator in Texas, and Gina Hinojosa, the state's new governor, toast their successes

Talarico's supporters went beserk as the projected results arrived at an election-night party, wrote the BBC's Gary O'Donoghue. Attendees hugged, kissed and drank to a reality they dared not believe would come true.

Hinojosa became the first Latina to be elected governor of the state.

Among the other states where history was made by the Democrats were Kansas, where Adam Hamilton took a closely-fought Senate seat - giving the state its first Democratic senator for nearly a century.

REACTION: Trump accepts Democrats did a 'good job' in election

ANALYSIS: Democrats push deep into Trump heartland on historic night

Senior party figures who cheered the results included Hakeem Jeffries, the top Democrat in the House of Representatives, who is now poised to take over as the Speaker of the lower chamber of Congress.

"Today begins the great American comeback," he told a crowd of supporters.

If Jeffries' colleagues choose to keep him as the leader of their party's delegation in House, he will be the next Speaker, taking over from Republican Mike Johnson and becoming the first black person to fill that role.

Trump spoke throughout the evening to a number of US media outlets, striking a conciliatory tone and conceding that the Democrats did a "good job".

He said he would accept the results and that he was looking forward to working with the opposition party - albeit he would still have the power, as president, to veto legislation that Democrats advance through Texas.

Trump distanced himself from some of the Republican candidates who contested the election, saying: "In some cases they didn't like the candidates, and in some cases those candidates were not my friend."

He acknowledged that the US-Israel war on Iran, which has impacted consumer prices in the US, had affected the vote. Trump argued that high fuel prices meant voters could not see how well his economy was doing "as much as they should have", adding: "The gasoline was caused by Iran."

Abdul El-Sayed has become the first Muslim person elected to the US Senate - after claiming victory in Michigan in what was one of a handful of closely fought contests. His win means the seat stays in Democratic hands.

He defeated Republican opponent Mike Rogers, who received the surprise backing of El-Sayed's Democratic rival in the primary contest, Haley Stevens, who changed her endorsement at the eleventh hour.

The race was one of the bitterest of the midterms. Trump invoked 9/11 and even called El-Sayed a "jihadist" without citing any evidence, prompting a backlash and accusations of Islamophobia.

El-Sayed triumphed in one of the bitterest contests of the midterms

El-Sayed's supporters went wild when he took the stage to celebrate his claimed victory, wrote the BBC's Nada Tawfik. He said: "Up until right about now, you might have noticed I always say 'inshallah,' or 'God willing,' whenever the outcome of this race was mentioned."

He went on to say: "But today, tonight, we can turn that 'inshallah' into 'alhamdulillah'. For the right-wing ops, that just means 'praise be to God.'"

The defeat of Susan Collins, who has served as the Republican senator for Maine for three decades, was one indicator of the scale of the Republican losses.

She was felled by Democratic candidate Troy Jackson, a logger who sought to point to his background to persuade blue-collar Mainers to vote for him.

In a statement reported by US media, Collins conceded defeat and said it had been the "honour of my life" to serve the state for 30 years.

The BBC's Helena Humphrey described a sombre atmosphere at Collins's watch party in the city of Maine - where the song Closing Time was played.

There was a sombre atmosphere at the watch party for Republican Susan Collins

Collins entered the race as one of the most vulnerable incumbent Republicans seeking re-election, and found that her tactic to distance herself from Trump did not work.

Ashley Hinson suffered the same fate in Iowa, losing that closely-fought Senate seat to Josh Turek. Hinson had slightly distanced herself from the president, urging him to end the war with Iran and breaking with him on easing beef tariffs, while at the same time defending Trump's broader tariff policies as important.

Among the other firsts of the night were in Florida, where Republican Byron Donalds has become the state's first black governor, following a hearty endorsement from Trump.

Donalds, who will take over the job from Ron DeSantis, said his state was the "envy of the nation", and that he would work to make the state more affordable.

The result was one of the few high points of an otherwise difficult evening for the Republicans.

A win for Bryon Donalds in Florida was one of the few high points for Republicans

In the neighbouring state of Georgia, Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms, the former mayor of Atlanta, won the governor's race, becoming the first black governor and first woman to be elected to the state's highest office.

The BBC's Carl Nasman described ecstatic scenes as the race was projected blue. Bottoms defeated Republican Rick Jackson, a billionaire businessman and healthcare executive.

The state has also not had a Democratic governor for more than 20 years.

Elsewhere, Democrat Chris Pappas became the first openly gay man elected to the Senate. He celebrated on stage alongside his husband in New Hampshire.

Pappas defeated former Republican Senator John Sununu in the race, whom he thanked on Tuesday, saying: "We all owe John Sununu a big debt of gratitude for his public service for the people of New Hampshire."

The 46-year-old Pappas had represented New Hampshire in the House since 2019 but chose not to run for re-election so he could launch his campaign for the Senate.

Watch: "I am ready to fight" - Senator-elect Chris Pappas celebrates New Hampshire win

#81. First 10 Australian songs inducted into the National Songbook after ‘very robust’ judging

Yothu Yindi’s Treaty, Midnight Oil’s Beds Are Burning and Helen Reddy’s I Am Woman are among the first songs to be honoured on new list that will grow every year

Warning: Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander readers are advised that this article contains images and names of Indigenous Australians who have died

Midnight Oil’s Beds Are Burning, Yothu Yindi’s Treaty and Helen Reddy’s I Am Woman are among the first 10 Australian songs to be inducted into the National Songbook – a new digital archive that celebrates and preserves songs that have shaped Australia’s national cultural identity.

Launched on Wednesday night at the National Film and Sound Archive (NFSA) in Canberra, the songbook is a joint initiative between NFSA and the music rights organisation Apra Amcos. The first 10 songs were selected by a panel of 11 music experts including broadcasters Richard Kingsmill and Zan Rowe, composer Cornel Wilczek and musician Mo’Ju.

The Apra chair, Jenny Morris, and the NFSA chair, Annette Shun Wah, facilitated a “very robust conversation”, said Meagan Loader, the chief curator of the NFSA.

“There’s no such thing as a definitive list when it comes to music – you can never capture everyone’s taste – but I hope people see at least one song on there and see themselves reflected in the list,” said Loader, who was not part of the panel. “You hear some of these songs and you feel distinctly that they say something about who we are as a nation.”

The oldest song on the list is Andrew “Banjo” Paterson’s Waltzing Matilda, written by the bush poet in 1895 and later composed by Christina Macpherson. The 1927 recording “remains one of Australia’s most enduring hauntings”, the panel said.

Yothu Yindi’s Treaty – written by Dr M Yunupingu, Stuart Kellaway, Witiyana Marika, M Mununggurr, Cal Williams, G Yunupingu, Peter Garrett and Paul Kelly – was included for being a “landmark protest anthem”. Written in 1990 around a campfire, the rallying song has soundtracked generations of gatherings, from protests to dancefloors.

“I’m proud to be part of the song,” Paul Kelly told Guardian Australia. “Like the song, the process of treaty-making around the country is the work of many hands and still ongoing.”

Kelly said the song came to be when he and M Yunupingu were sitting around a campfire at Birany Birany, East Arnhem Land.

“From there it travelled through many hands – to a Darwin rehearsal room with the rest of the band, then to recording studios in Melbourne and Sydney, a hotel room in Glebe, the Midnight Oil offices with Peter Garrett, and back to Melbourne again,” he said.

Melbourne electronic producers Filthy Lucre had a “huge influence” on the track, Kelly said. “[They] took a minor hit and thoroughly re-tooled it, stripping out many of its elements, including most of the English vocals, and turning it into an international dancefloor banger that really put Yothu Yindi on the map.”

The most recent Australian song on the list is another call to arms disguised as an electronic banger: 2008’s My People by the Presets. Written by Julian Hamilton and Kim Moyes, the track is a protest song about Australia’s treatment of asylum seekers. Its lyrics – “I’m here with all of my people / locked up with all of my people” – were written as a declaration of solidarity. “Its political message may be less immediately apparent to listeners today, but its ferocity remains,” the panel said.

The Easybeats’ 1966 garage-rock anthem Friday on My Mind – written by founding members Harry Vanda and George Young – was honoured for capturing the “restless energy of a generation hungry for romance and escape”.

Ruby Hunter’s 1990 song Down City Streets, initially recorded for Archie Roach’s album Charcoal Lane, about displacement as a member of the Stolen Generations, and Helen Reddy’s feminist anthem I Am Woman were also included. Both songs were praised by the panel for their personal storytelling and for resonating with listeners after their lifetimes.

Neil Murray’s My Island Home, released by the Warumpi Band in 1987 and covered by Christine Anu in 1995, was chosen for its evolution over time and “enduring pull of home and belonging”.

Loader was personally stoked to see Midnight Oil’s Beds Are Burning – written by Peter Garrett, Rob Hirst and Jim Moginie – on the list. “I remember being a child and hearing it for the first time and understanding Australian history and culture in a whole new way,” she said. “It was almost an awakening.

“For those messages to still be so powerful and important [years later], to me that speaks volumes to the power of incredible songwriting.”

The first 10 songs are not all commercial releases. The score to 1998 film The Boys, written by avant garde jazz trio the Necks, was included for its “unsettling” and “off-kilter” qualities. And Peter Sculthorpe’s 11-minute Earth Cry, featuring William Barton playing the yidaki (didgeridoo), made the list for its ability to capture “the vastness of Australia”.

The list will continue to grow and evolve each year, with a new panel appointed to select 10 more songs.

To be eligible, the songs must have been written or co-written by an Australian Apra member, be at least a decade old, and have a demonstrable cultural impact.

“We see this first list as a great conversation starter,” Loader said. “There were a couple of commercially successful, huge songs that were a shoo-in for this list, but we decided we would let the songbook grow over time and become a conversation about what is a really significant Australian song … It’ll be really interesting to see in 10 years’ time what that songbook will look like.”

Friday on My Mind by the Easybeats (1966), written by Harry Vanda and George Young

I Am Woman by Helen Reddy, written by Reddy and Ray Burton (1971)

My Island Home, written by Neil Murray and released by Warumpi Band (1987) and Christine Anu (1995)

#79. How America voted in maps and charts

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

It's been an extraordinary night for the Democrats in the US midterms.

They have taken control of the Senate and the House of Representatives too.

The results in the Senate are beyond what the Democrats would have expected. North Carolina was a likely pick-up but South Carolina was a huge shock and Kansas had not returned a Democratic senator since 1932.

Texas, Iowa, Ohio and Maine have also turned blue and the Democrats held Michigan. There is one toss-up seat to come in Alaska and even Mississippi is now on a knife edge, according to CBS News.

Polls before the election saw South Carolina as leaning Republican. It had been a deep red seat for many years, last returning a Democratic senator in 1998.

The shift from Republican to Democrat happened across the country. For example recent vote share shows Texas moved 9 points towards the Democrats when compared with the 2024 Senate race.

The BBC's partner CBS News projected the Democrats would win the House just after 03:00 GMT on Wednesday (22:00 EST Tuesday).

Now it's a question of how big that win will be as counting continues.

Two significant seats are Florida 14 and Florida 25 which the Democrats have held. This is a blow to the Republicans' attempt to redraw the boundaries in their favour.

The redistricting saw both districts move from having a majority of 2024 Kamala Harris voters to having a majority of people who voted for Donald Trump.

Florida 22, which was also redrawn to favour the Republicans, saw a victory for the Democrats too.

A huge poll of voters suggests women turned out in numbers and most voted Democrat. Men on the other hand were evenly split between the two main parties.

Hispanic voters look to be one of the key groups in the elections in Florida. The poll suggests this group backed the Democratic Senate candidate Angie Nixon by 55% to 44% for the Republican Ashley Moody.

This is a turnaround from the estimated 55% of Hispanic voters who backed Donald Trump in 2024.

The poll also asked what issues voters were most concerned about. It suggests that across the country the economy was the biggest issue, followed by the state of democracy.

When voters were asked if Donald Trump's policies impacted economic conditions, the poll suggests more that two-thirds believe they had made things worse.

#77. Aukus is ‘worst, riskiest and most costly’ Australian defence decision ever, public inquiry into submarine deal says

Report led by Peter Garrett and former defence chief Adm Chris Barrie says agreement should be suspended and calls for royal commission

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

A public inquiry into the $368bn Aukus nuclear submarine agreement has called the plan “the worst, riskiest and most costly defence decision ever made by an Australian government”, urging its immediate termination.

Former Labor MP and Midnight Oil frontman Peter Garrett, alongside former chief of defence Adm Chris Barrie, led the inquiry dominated by critics of the deal with the UK and US, recommending the suspension of plans for new bases and for a royal commission to be held into the agreement signed by the former Morrison government.

Its report, titled Surrendering Australia: the Aukus Deception, says Australia risks being dragged into military conflict with China because of the presence of powerful nuclear submarines. Aukus badly undermines Australia’s sovereignty and undercuts longstanding commitments to nuclear non-proliferation, it says.

The inquiry report, released on Wednesday, was downloaded nearly 15,000 times within an hour of being published online.

The report calls for a rework of the second pillar of the agreement, which covers technology and defence capability sharing, and for a fundamental recalibration of Australia’s relationship with Beijing.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

After hearings around the country and more than 1,000 public submissions, the inquiry highlighted a lack of scrutiny and oversight over the five years since Aukus was announced and called for the auditor general and federal parliament to launch independent reviews.

“Since coming to power, Labor has failed to conduct a thorough review of this most significant of defence agreements,” Garrett told the National Press Club in Canberra.

“Conceived in secret, Aukus has spurned established defence and parliamentary oversight practices.”

He said the Stirling defence precinct in Perth risked becoming a “de facto US base” hosting as many as 2,000 serving American personnel, and that nearby communities including Rockingham and Fremantle would become targets for attack.

“The Aukus submarine plan is not tenable,” Barrie, who served as chief of defence between 1998 and 2002, said.

“It’s time for our government to take the lead on this matter. Scrap the Aukus submarine plan now, and rebuild national security decision-making processes.”

Labor agreed to support the deal for Australia to acquire nuclear submarines, which was negotiated under Morrison, then US president Joe Biden and UK prime minister Boris Johnson. As part of the agreement, Australia is funding upgrades to the US defence industrial base and is due to start receiving secondhand Virginia class nuclear submarines around 2032.

But delays and cost blowouts for production of submarines for the US navy have led to scepticism that Australia will receive the in-service boats.

The UK parliament held a year-long review and, after an inquiry by the Pentagon in 2025, Donald Trump agreed to support Aukus.

But former prime ministers Paul Keating and Malcolm Turnbull, former foreign affairs ministers Gareth Evans and Bob Carr, environmental and security campaigners, and some within Labor believe Aukus is not in Australia’s national interest.

Frustrated by the lack of scrutiny – in part due to bipartisan support for Aukus in federal parliament – unions, not-for-profits and former officeholders launched their own inquiry backed by public donations totalling $220,000.

Labor argues Aukus was considered as part of the government’s defence strategic review.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

Government ministers have dismissed the public inquiry as a stitch-up by critics of the agreement. A spokesperson for the defence minister, Richard Marles, said on Wednesday that Aukus is a core pillar of the Albanese government’s national security policy.

“As the national defence strategy 2026 re-confirms, we confront the most challenging strategic circumstances since the second world war, and we do so at a time where Australia’s economic connection with the world has never been greater,” they said.

“We need to support the development of an ADF with a much greater capacity to project so we can defend Australia and contribute to regional security and stability.”

Garrett warned any certainty the US president would eventually agree to send submarines to Australia could not be guaranteed.

“There are no refunds, no enforceable obligations on partner countries, and no recourse if things go wrong,” he said.

The inquiry estimates an additional $180bn will be needed to deal with nuclear waste generated by Aukus, based on evidence to the inquiry. No official cost estimates for waste have been published.

Australia has not identified a permanent storage site for the waste, including the high-level radioactive waste from the reactor core and spent fuel, which will remain toxic for thousands of years.

The government’s preferred measure of the total cost of the deal is 0.15% of GDP over its lifetime.

The first Virginia-class nuclear sub from the US is due to arrive in Australia in 2032, with another arriving every four years, before the Australian-built model is ready for operations. The bespoke SSN Aukus model is due to come online in 2042.

Campaign group Labor Against War welcomed the report.

“With rising cost of living and unemployment, spiralling inflation and interest rates, it is beyond comprehension why the Australian government is throwing billions into the war chests of the US military at a time when the Australian people are crying out for solutions at home,” the group’s patron and a former senator, Doug Cameron, said.

#73. ‘Not been this excited in a long time’: US expats ride the blue wave at Australian midterms watch parties

US voters, their Australian partners and British spectators crowd around pub TVs to watch the historic election results roll in

Democrats’ blue wave and a bad night for Trump: key US midterms takeaways

On the second floor of a Sydney pub, the mood shifts from nervous, browser-refreshing anxiety to champagne-popping within a matter of hours.

More than 100 US expats and election enthusiasts have packed into the function space at the Keg and Brew in the inner-east suburb of Surry Hills to watch the midterms results roll in.

They’ve followed a sign reading “Democrats Abroad” guiding them upstairs at Wednesday lunchtime as live reports began broadcasting on the pub television screens.

Early on, the American voters, their Australian partners and British spectators said they are “cautiously optimistic”.

Within a couple of hours, news of the Democrats’ historic win dramatically shifts the mood.

Katrena Henderson, from North Carolina, says she has “not been this excited about an election in a long time”.

Sitting with her Australian husband and adult son, the trio compulsively refresh their phones for results and celebrate when former governor Roy Cooper flips her state’s Senate seat to the Democrats.

“Nobody, I think, really believed that Democrats were going to do it. And they won decisively,” she says. “It just really points to the fact that Americans want something very different, because this administration is not working for us.

“I think what we didn’t have before today was a lot of hope … and at least now there is some hope.”

David Smith, an associate professor at the University of Sydney’s United States Studies Centre, says the midterms are a “referendum on Trump”. He says the writing was on the wall as voters went to the polls, with the president’s approval ratings at a “historically low” level.

“All year, I think Americans have been frustrated by Trump’s lack of focus on things that matter to most Americans,” he says.

“Whether that’s the war in Iran, which most Americans didn’t want and which have sent prices up in a pretty direct way, or whether it’s Trump’s constant beautification projects in Washington DC … All of this is just evidence to a lot of Americans that he is focused on the wrong things and he’s focused on his own self-aggrandisement rather than the country.”

Cindy Herman-Jones moved to Australia from Texas almost 15 years ago and has never cast a vote while living abroad. She says she was compelled to mail in a ballot because “the stakes of this election” were too high.

“Democracy itself is being attacked by the Trump regime,” she says. “I just feel like they’ve done so many terrible things and, if he’s in power for another two years with no check on that, then it’s just going to continue to get worse and worse.”

In a list of the “many terrible things” to come out of Trump’s presidency, Herman-Jones points to the increased “brutality” of ICE immigration enforcement and the US war in Iran – which she calls “the worst thing” he’s done.

Her Senate vote for James Talarico was one of many that helped him flip the Texas upper house seat for the Democrats. She was “amazed” at the result, she says, because “it’s such a hard job for a Democrat to win in Texas”.

Aliana Hensel is a first-time voter. The 18-year-old Californian has lived in Australia for about eight years with her American parents.

She says that while her parents “don’t generally vote” and have not done so since moving abroad, it was something she wanted to do because “something really, really needs to be done” about US leadership.

“I’ve been watching politics closely for the last few years. It’s something I feel really passionate about. I wasn’t old enough to vote in the presidential election, but I watched that, and it was quite the disappointment,” she says. “So I’ve been very focused on knowing that I can vote this time and wanting to make a bit of an impact.”

Just a couple of kilometres to the north of the Keg and Brew, a small group of election watchers gathered at the Cheers pub off Sydney’s George Street in the CBD over schnitzels and pints of Guinness.

The event was posted as a meetup for some Republican expat groups, but attracted more of a mixed bag of libertarians and independents interested in the drama of an American election.

“It’s interesting to me to see how the populations in America are responding to their situation,” Rahn Wood, 60, an Australian who splits his time between Sydney and Vietnam, said. When asked if he thought Australia and other nations looked towards the US when it came to political decisions, Wood said Donald Trump’s influence had a long reach.

“I think in a policy sense it does,” he said. “You see that with the green agenda and the support of the United Nations, both of those things are waning because the US – whether it’s Donald Trump or the Congress itself – isn’t very supportive of some of those agendas.

“I think that gives permission, structure for other people to say: ‘Actually it’s not working for us either,’” he added. “This election … from a policy point of view, does really set the direction for western countries, I think.”

Wood adds he doesn’t think Australians are interested in electing anybody as mercurial as Trump “but, by the same token, there’s obviously a big chunk of people that are looking for change”.

Others were not optimistic about the results handed down on Fox News, with one woman saying she was watching the growing blue wave with dread. Many wandered off after lunch.

Smith says while the election “could have significant ramifications as far as things like the Iran war go”, he doubts it will directly influence Australia or Albanese’s relationship to the US because “congress, at the moment, doesn’t have a very big role in foreign policy”.

Smith believes the most interesting is what happens to “Trumpism”.

“There are lots of politicians around the world who take their cues from Trump, including in Australia … If this is an election that really shows the limits of Trumpism in the United States, that could potentially make a few rightwing politicians worldwide rethink how much they want to be tied to Trump,” he says.

#62. Beijing Queer film festival cancelled after harassment from authorities

Forums for discussion of feminist and LGBTQ+ views face growing intolerance from Chinese state, say experts

Beijing’s annual independent queer film festival has been cancelled after what is understood to have been a campaign of sustained harassment from authorities.

Beijing Queer film festival, which was due to begin on Friday in China’s capital, is one of the country’s oldest independent film festivals.

In a statement posted on Instagram late on Thursday, the organisers said: “We regret to inform you that the Love Queer Film Week … has been cancelled. We sincerely apologise for any inconvenience and disappointment that the cancellation of this event may cause to those who planned to participate or had been looking forward to the film week.”

Police had increased pressure on the festival through repeated phone calls in recent weeks, it is understood.

Founded in 2001 by a group of Peking University students, the festival grew to become the only film festival in mainland China to offer a public forum for films and discussions exploring LGBTQ+ issues.

The Chinese authorities have grown increasingly intolerant of open discussion of feminist or LGBTQ+ issues. Film festivals in particular attract particular scrutiny because they provide an opportunity to platform narratives that sit outside the Communist party’s worldview.

Beijing Independent film festival, China’s most prominent independent film festival, was closed down in 2014 after the authorities detained the founders and shut off the electricity supply to the venue. Last year, an independent Chinese film festival that was due to be held in New York was cancelled after harassment from the Chinese authorities, an incident which raised concern about transnational repression.

Maya Wang, the deputy Asia director for Human Rights Watch, said: “The Chinese government under Xi has been relentlessly tightening social and ideological control, and so any activities outside of those sanctioned by the party-state are subjected to intensifying scrutiny and often also official harassment.

“The authorities have put more pressure on what little is left of China’s civil society, as well as the LGBT communities and independent film-makers. A queer film festival basically faces all of these overlapping pressures.”

Hongwei Bao, a professor of Chinese studies at the University of St Andrews who focuses on LGBT issues, said that the festival was “a beacon for LGBT resilience” in China.

“The shutdown of the Love Queer Cinema Week has a chilling affect on the Chinese queer community,” Bao said.

In June this year, screenings of films with LGBTQ+ themes that were scheduled to be held in Beijing during Pride month at the Institut Français, a French cultural centre, were cancelled because of police harassment.

Despite the increased pressure, Beijing Queer film festival remains a popular event. Several international film-makers whose work had been selected for the festival had travelled to China to participate. Last year, the festival received 200 volunteer applications, four times the number received in 2024.

Beijing’s public security bureau could not be reached for comment.
